g_{k}(m)\to f(m)
. We now extend
g
to
[0,1]
by setting
g(x)=f(m)
for all
x\in[m,1]
. Then
f=g
on
A
, and for all
x\in[0,m)
, we have
g(x)=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}g_{k_{\ell}}(x)\leq\lim_{\ell\to\infty}g_{k_{\ell}}(m)=f(m)
, so
g\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
.

•

If condition (ii) holds, then
M_{\nu}^{R}\in(0,m)
is an isolated point of
\supp\nu
, so
\nu(\{M_{\nu}^{R}\})>0
,
M_{\nu}^{R}\in A
and
M_{\nu}^{\prime}:=\max(\supp\nu\setminus\{M_{\nu}^{R}\})<M_{\nu}^{R}<m
. Let
h\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be the function that agrees with
f
on
[0,M_{\nu}^{\prime}]\cup\{M_{\nu}^{R}\}
and is linear on
[M_{\nu}^{\prime},1]
.
Then
h
is linear on
[m,1]
and convex and increasing on
[0,1]
, so
h\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
. Since
(M_{\nu}^{\prime},1]\cap A=\{M_{\nu}^{R}\}
, the functions
f,g,h
agree on
A
, as required.

The analogous case where
\supp\nu\subseteq[m,1]
can be handled in much the same way, and so we have now demonstrated the sufficiency of each of the conditions (i), (ii) and (iii).

(c,
\Rightarrow
) Supposing that none of the conditions (i)–(iii) hold, we now verify that
\Cl\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}\supsetneqq\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}
. We consider only the cases where
\supp\nu\subseteq[0,m]
; the arguments are similar if
\supp\nu\subseteq[m,1]
.

Case 1 –
\nu(\{M_{\nu}^{R}\})=0
: Note that there exists
f\in L^{2}(\nu)
such that
\left.f\right|_{E_{\nu}}
is convex and increasing, and
f(x)\to\infty
as
x\nearrow M_{\nu}^{R}
. Indeed, a concrete example of such a function can be obtained via the following construction: since
M_{\nu}^{R}
is not an isolated point of
\supp\nu
by assumption, there exists a sequence
(a_{n}\in\supp\nu\setminus\{M_{\nu}^{R}\}:n\in\mathbb{N})
such that
a_{n}\nearrow M_{\nu}^{R}
and
\nu((a_{n},M_{\nu}^{R}))\leq 2^{-3n}
for all
n
. For each
n
, let
h_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be such that
h_{n}=0
on
[0,a_{n}]
,
h_{n}(M_{\nu}^{R})=2^{n/2}
and
h_{n}
is linear on
[a_{n},1]
. Then
h_{n}
is convex and increasing, and
\|h_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\nu)}\leq(2^{n}\cdot 2^{-3n})^{1/2}=2^{-n}
. Thus, the function
h\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
h(x):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}(x)\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<M_{\nu}^{R}\}}
is also convex and increasing. Moreover,
\|h\|_{L^{2}(\nu)}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|h_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\nu)}<\infty
and
h(x)\to\infty
as
x\nearrow M_{\nu}^{R}
.

For any
f
with the above properties, we now argue that
[f]_{\nu}\notin\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}
, i.e. that there does not exist
g\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
such that
f\sim_{\nu}g
. Indeed, if
g
is a function that agrees with
f
on a set
S\subseteq\supp\nu
with the property that
\nu(S^{c}\cap\supp\nu)=0
, then recall from the second paragraph of the proof that
S
is dense in
\supp\nu
. Thus, since
M_{\nu}^{R}
is not an isolated point of
\supp\nu
, there exists a sequence
(s_{n}\in S\setminus\{M_{\nu}^{R}\}:n\in\mathbb{N})
such that
s_{n}\to M_{\nu}^{R}
, and we must have
g(s_{n})=f(s_{n})
for all
n
. But this implies that
g(x)\to\infty
as
x\nearrow M_{\nu}^{R}
, so
g
cannot be extended to a finite convex function on
[0,m]
.

Case 2 –
\nu(\{M_{\nu}^{R}\})>0
: Consider any
f\in L^{2}(\nu)
such that
\left.f\right|_{E_{\nu}}
is convex and increasing, and
f
is discontinuous at
M_{\nu}^{R}
. Since
M_{\nu}^{R}\in(0,m)
is not an isolated point of
\supp\nu
, we deduce as before that if
f\sim_{\nu}g
for some
g\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, then there exists a sequence
(s_{n}\in\supp\nu\setminus\{M_{\nu}^{R}\}:n\in\mathbb{N})
such that
s_{n}\to M_{\nu}^{R}
and
g(s_{n})=f(s_{n})
for all
n
. But since
\nu(\{M_{\nu}^{R}\})>0
, we have
g(M_{\nu}^{R})=f(M_{\nu}^{R})\neq\lim_{n\to\infty}f(s_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}g(s_{n})
, so
g
is not continuous at
M_{\nu}^{R}\in(0,m)
and hence does not belong to
\mathcal{F}^{m}
. This shows that
[f]_{\nu}\notin\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}
and hence that
\Cl\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}\supsetneqq\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}
, as required.

(d) Suppose again that none of the conditions (i)–(iii) hold, assuming for the time being that
\supp\nu\subseteq[0,m]
. Then
m>0
and
M_{\nu}^{R}\in[0,m]
is not an isolated point of
\supp\nu
. Let
(f_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
be any sequence in
\mathcal{F}^{m}
such that
\|f_{n}-f\|_{L^{2}(\nu)}\to 0
for some
f\in L^{2}(\nu)
. By a very similar argument to that given in the first bullet point in Case 2 of (c,
\Leftarrow
), there exists an increasing convex
g
defined on
E_{\nu}
that agrees
\nu
-almost everywhere with
f
; recall that
E_{\nu}
contains
M_{\nu}^{R}
if and only if
\nu(\{M_{\nu}^{R}\})>0
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}=\{[f]_{\nu}:f\in\mathcal{F}^{m}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(\nu)
by (a), we deduce that

\Cl\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}\subseteq\{[f]_{\nu}:f\in L^{2}(\nu)\text{ and}\left.f\right|_{E_{\nu}}\!\text{ is convex and increasing}\}.

For the reverse inclusion, we split into the two cases considered in (c,
\Rightarrow
) above.

Case 1 –
\nu(\{M_{\nu}^{R}\})=0
: Here, we have
M_{\nu}^{R}\notin E_{\nu}
. For a fixed
f\in L^{2}(\nu)
such that
\left.f\right|_{E_{\nu}}
is convex and increasing, we claim that there exists a sequence
(f_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{F}^{m}
such that
\|f_{n}-f\|_{L^{2}(\nu)}\to 0
. Indeed, fix a sequence
(x_{n}\in\supp\nu\setminus\{M_{\nu}^{R}\}:n\in\mathbb{N})
such that
x_{n}\nearrow M_{\nu}^{R}
, and for each
n
, observe that since
\left.f\right|_{E_{\nu}}
has a finite and non-negative subgradient at
x_{n}
, there exists an increasing convex
f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
such that
f_{n}=f
on
E_{\nu}\cap[0,x_{n}]
,
f_{n}
is linear on
[x_{n},1]
and
f_{n}\leq f
on
E_{\nu}
. Thus, since
\nu(E_{\nu})=1
,
\inf_{E_{\nu}}f\leq f_{n}\leq\sup_{E_{\nu}}f
on
E_{\nu}
for all
n
and
f_{n}\to f
pointwise on
E_{\nu}
, it follows by the dominated convergence theorem that
\|f_{n}-f\|_{L^{2}(\nu)}^{2}=\int_{E_{\nu}}\,\lvert f_{n}-f\rvert^{2}\to 0
, as required.

Case 2 –
\nu(\{M_{\nu}^{R}\})>0
: Note that
M_{\nu}^{R}<m
and
M_{\nu}^{R}\in E_{\nu}
in this case. As before, take any
f\in L^{2}(\nu)
such that
\left.f\right|_{E_{\nu}}
is convex and increasing, and fix a sequence
(x_{n}\in\supp\nu\setminus\{M_{\nu}^{R}\}:n\in\mathbb{N})
such that
x_{n}\nearrow M_{\nu}^{R}
. For each
n
, let
f_{n}
be the (unique) function that satisfies
f_{n}=f
on
[0,x_{n}]\cup\{M_{\nu}^{R}\}
and is linear on
[x_{n},1]
. Then
f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
for all
n
by the convexity of
f
, and
f_{n}\to f
pointwise on
E_{\nu}
. Thus, since
\inf_{E_{\nu}}f\leq f_{n}\leq f(M_{\nu}^{R})<\infty
on
E_{\nu}
for all
n
, we can once again apply the dominated convergence theorem to deduce that
\|f_{n}-f\|_{L^{2}(\nu)}^{2}=\int_{E_{\nu}}\,\lvert f_{n}-f\rvert^{2}\to 0
. This shows that
\Cl\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{m}\supseteq\{[f]_{\nu}:f\in L^{2}(\nu)\text{ and}\left.f\right|_{E_{\nu}}\!\text{ is convex and increasing}\}
in this case.

Straightforward modifications of the arguments above yield the analogous conclusion when
\supp\nu\subseteq[m,1]
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
S10
.

(a) For
f\in L^{2}(P^{X})
, it is immediate from (
S34
) that
f\in\psi_{m}^{*}(P)
if and only if
[f]_{P^{X}}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(P^{X})
is the projection of
[f_{P}]_{P^{X}}
onto
\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m}
, which is a closed, convex subset of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{L}^{2}(P^{X})
by Proposition
S9
.

(b) This follows directly from the definition of
\psi_{m}^{*}(P)
and the fact that every
f\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
is bounded on
[0,1]
.

(c) Since
\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m}=\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{\tilde{m}}
by Proposition
S9
(b), we have
\psi_{m}^{*}(P)=\psi_{\tilde{m}}^{*}(P)
by definition, and
L_{m}^{*}(P)=L_{\tilde{m}}^{*}(P)
by the observation after (
S34
). If there exists
f\in\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
, then setting
\tilde{f}(x):=f(M_{L}\vee x\wedge M_{R})
for
x\in[0,1]
with
M_{L}:=\min(\supp P^{X})
and
M_{R}:=\max(\supp P^{X})
, we have
\tilde{f}\in\psi_{\tilde{m}}^{0}(P)
.

(d) If condition (i) holds, then
\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m}=\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m}
by Proposition
S9
(c). Thus, there exists
f^{*}\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
such that
\psi^{*}(P)=[f^{*}]_{P^{X}}
by (a) above, whence
f^{*}\in\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
.

Suppose now that condition (ii) holds, in which case
m=\tilde{m}
and there exist a regression function
f_{P}
for
P
and
b,\varepsilon>0
such that
\lvert f_{P}\rvert\leq b
on
(m-\varepsilon,m+\varepsilon)
. We may assume without loss of generality that
m=\max(\supp P^{X})
; the case
m=\min(\supp P^{X})
is similar. Suppose for a contradiction that
\psi_{m}^{0}(P)=\emptyset
, i.e. that
\psi^{*}(P)\in\bigl(\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m}\bigr)\setminus\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m}
. In view of condition (i) in Proposition
S9
(c), this can only happen if
P^{X}(\{m\})=0
. By Proposition
S9
(d), we can write
\psi_{m}^{*}(P)=[f]_{P^{X}}
for some
f\in L^{2}(P^{X})
that is convex and increasing on
{\Int(\csupp P^{X})}
. Since
[f]_{P^{X}}=\psi_{m}^{*}(P)\notin\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m}
, the function
\left.f\right|_{\Int(\csupp P^{X})}
cannot be extended to an element of
\mathcal{F}^{m}
, so we must have
f(x)\to\infty
as
x\nearrow m
.

Therefore, we can find
m^{\prime}\in\Int(\csupp P^{X})\cap(m-\varepsilon,m)
such that
f(x)>b
for all
x\in[m^{\prime},m)
. Since

\left.f\right|_{\Int(\csupp P^{X})}
has a finite and non-negative subgradient at
m^{\prime}
, there exists an increasing convex
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
such that
\tilde{f}=f
on
\Int(\csupp P^{X})\cap[0,m^{\prime}]
,
\tilde{f}
is linear on
[m^{\prime},1]
and
f_{P}\leq b<\tilde{f}\leq f
on
[m^{\prime},m)
. But this means that
\|\tilde{f}-f_{P}\|_{L^{2}(P^{X})}<\|f-f_{P}\|_{L^{2}(P^{X})}
, so
L_{m}^{*}(P)\leq L(\tilde{f},P)<L(f,P)
by (
S34
), contradicting the fact that
f\in\psi_{m}^{*}(P)
. Thus,
\psi_{m}^{*}(P)\in\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m}
, whence
\psi_{m}^{0}(P)\neq\emptyset
.

(e) If
f,g\in\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
, then
f\sim_{P^{X}}g
by (a), so
f=g
on some dense subset
S\subseteq\supp P^{X}
; see the first paragraph of the proof of Proposition
S9
(c).
It follows that
f=g
on
(\supp P^{X})\setminus\{m\}
, a set on which both
f,g
are continuous. If
f,g
are both continuous on
[0,1]
, then
f=g
on
\supp P^{X}
by the same argument. If in addition
P^{X}(\{m\})>0
, then clearly
f(m)=g(m)
.

(f) The forward implication was established in (e). For the converse, suppose that
m\in\supp P^{X}
and there is some
f\in\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
that is discontinuous at
m
, so that
\lim_{x\nearrow m}f(x)<\lim_{x\searrow m}f(x)
. If
P^{X}(\{m\})=0
, then any
\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
contains any
\tilde{f}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\tilde{f}=f
on
[0,1]\setminus\{m\}
and
\lim_{x\nearrow m}f(x)\leq\tilde{f}(m)\leq\lim_{x\searrow m}f(x)
, so the elements of
\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
do not all agree at
m\in\supp P^{X}
.

(g) Suppose that
\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
contains a continuous function
h\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
. For any other
f\in\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
, we know from (e) that
f=h
on
(\supp P^{X})\setminus\{m\}
. In view of the continuity of
h
at
m
and the assumption that
\supp P^{X}
has non-empty intersection with both
(m-\varepsilon,m)
and
(m,m+\varepsilon)
for all
\varepsilon>0
, this forces
f(m)=h(m)
, so
f=h
on
\supp P^{X}
and
f
is continuous.
∎

The proof of Proposition
S12
relies on the following three key lemmas. Let the marginal distribution
P^{X}
on
[0,1]
be as in Proposition
S12
, and define
M_{L}:=\min(\supp P^{X})
,
M_{R}:=\max(\supp P^{X})
and
C:=[M_{L},M_{R}]=\csupp P^{X}
.

Lemma S26
.

Fix
x\in\Int C
and
\ell\in[0,\infty)
. Let
(P_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathcal{P}
that converges weakly to some
P\in\mathcal{P}
. Then there exists
B\equiv B(x,\ell,P)<\infty
such that for any sequence of increasing functions
f_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\limsup_{n\to\infty}L(f_{n},P_{n})\leq\ell
for all
n
, we have
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,\lvert f_{n}(x)\rvert<B
.

Proof.

Since
x\in\Int C
, we have
P^{X}([0,x))\wedge P^{X}((x,1])>0
. Let
(f_{n})
be as above. Then for each
n
, note that since
f_{n}
is increasing, we have

L(f_{n},P_{n})\geq\int_{[x,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\,\bigl(y-f_{n}(x)\bigr)^{2}\,dP_{n}(x,y)\geq\left(\frac{f_{n}^{+}(x)}{2}\right)^{2}P_{n}\left([x,1]\times\left(-\infty,\frac{f_{n}^{+}(x)}{2}\right]\right);

(S69)

indeed, if
f_{n}(x)\leq 0
, then (
S69
) holds trivially, and if
f_{n}(x)>0
, then for all
x^{\prime}\in[x,1]
and
y^{\prime}\leq f_{n}(x)/2
, we have
f_{n}(x^{\prime})-y^{\prime}\geq f_{n}(x)-y^{\prime}\geq f_{n}(x)/2>0
.

Now let
(f_{n_{k}})
be a subsequence such that
f_{n_{k}}^{+}(x)\to\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}^{+}(x)=:2s
as
k\to\infty
. Since
P_{n}\overset{d}{\to}P
, an application of the portmanteau lemma
(
van der Vaart 1998
, Lemma 2.2)
shows that

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\,\left(\frac{f_{n_{k}}^{+}(x)}{2}\right)^{2}\,P_{n_{k}}\!\left((x,1]\times\left(-\infty,\frac{f_{n_{k}}^{+}(x)}{2}\right)\right)\geq s^{2}\,P\bigl((x,1]\times(-\infty,s)\bigr).

It follows from this and (
S69
) that
s^{2}\,P\bigl((x,1]\times(-\infty,s)\bigr)\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}L(f_{n},P_{n})\leq\ell
.
Since
P\bigl((x,1]\times(-\infty,b)\bigr)\to P^{X}((x,1])>0
as
b\to\infty
, we can therefore find
B^{\prime}\equiv B^{\prime}(x,\ell,P)<\infty
such that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,f_{n}^{+}(x)=2s<B^{\prime}
for any sequence
(f_{n})
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
S26
. An analogous argument yields the same conclusion for
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,f_{n}^{-}(x)
.
∎

For sequences of S-shaped functions, the conclusion of Lemma
S26
can be strengthened.

Lemma S27
.

Fix
\ell\in[0,\infty)
and let
(m_{n})
be a sequence in
[0,1]
that converges to some fixed
m_{0}\in\Int C
. Then under the hypotheses of Lemma
S26
, there exists
\tilde{B}\equiv\tilde{B}(m_{0},\ell,P)<\infty
such that for any sequence
(f_{n})
with
f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{n}}
for all
n
and
\limsup_{n\to\infty}L(f_{n},P_{n})\leq\ell
, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in[0,1]}\,\lvert f_{n}(x)\rvert<\tilde{B}
.

Proof.

Since
m_{n}\to m_{0}\in\Int C
, we can find
a_{L},a_{R}\in\Int C
such that
0<a_{L}<m_{0}<a_{R}<1
, so that for all sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
f_{n}(a_{L})\leq f_{n}(m_{n})\leq f_{n}(a_{R})
and

\frac{m_{n}f_{n}(a_{L})-a_{L}f_{n}(m_{n})}{m_{n}-a_{L}}\leq f_{n}(0)\leq f_{n}(x)\leq f_{n}(1)\leq\frac{(1-m_{n})f_{n}(m_{n})-(1-a_{R})f_{n}(a_{L})}{a_{R}-m_{n}}

(S70)

for all
x\in[0,1]
. Since
\limsup_{n\to\infty}L(f_{n},P_{n})\leq\ell
, we have
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,\lvert f_{n}(x)\rvert\leq B(x,\ell,P)<\infty
for
x\in\{a_{L},a_{R}\}
by Lemma
S26
, so the result follows from (
S70
) and the fact that
m_{n}\to m_{0}
.
∎

Another important consequence of Lemma
S26
is the following. Recall that
\tilde{m}_{0}=\argmin_{x\in C}\,\lvert x-m_{0}\rvert
.

Lemma S28
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
S12
, let
(f_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
be any sequence with
f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{n}}
and
\limsup_{n\to\infty}L(f_{n},P_{n})<\infty
for all
n
. Then for every subsequence
(g_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\equiv(f_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
, there is a further subsequence
(g_{k_{\ell}})
and a function
g
defined on
[0,1]
with the following properties:

(i)

g
is increasing on
C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
, convex on
C\cap[0,\tilde{m}_{0})
and concave on
C\cap(\tilde{m}_{0},1]
.

(ii)

g_{k_{\ell}}\to g
pointwise on
C
and uniformly on closed subsets of
C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
. In particular, if
z_{\ell}\to z\in C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
, then
g_{k_{\ell}}(z_{\ell})\to g(z)
.

(iii)

g(x)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
, and
g(\tilde{m}_{0})\in(-\infty,\infty]
if
\tilde{m}_{0}>M_{L}
and
g(\tilde{m}_{0})\in[-\infty,\infty)
if
\tilde{m}_{0}<M_{R}
.

(iv)

g\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{0}}
if
m_{0}\in\Int C
and
[g]_{P^{X}}\in\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(P^{X})
if
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
.

(v)

Let
Q_{\ell}:=P_{n_{k_{\ell}}}
for each
\ell
. If
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
, then
\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}L(g_{k_{\ell}},Q_{\ell})\geq L(g,P)\geq L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
.

(vi)

If
P_{n}=P
for all
n
, then we can ensure that the conclusions of (iii) and (iv) hold in all cases, even when the assumptions on
\tilde{m}_{0}
are dropped.

Proof.

Fix a subsequence
(g_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\equiv(f_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
. In the setting of Proposition
S12
, we have
m_{n_{k}}\to m_{0}
, so if
0\leq z<m_{0}<w\leq 1
, then all but finitely many of the functions
g_{k}
are convex on
[0,z]
and concave on
[w,1]
. Since
\{g_{k}(x)\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
is bounded for all
x\in\Int C
by Lemma
S26
, it follows from
Rockafellar 1997
and Lemma
S29
that whenever
0\leq z<m_{0}<w\leq 1
, there is a subsequence of
(g_{k})
that converges pointwise on
C\setminus(z,w)
. By considering sequences
z_{n}\nearrow m_{0}
and
w_{n}\searrow m_{0}
with
z_{n}<m_{0}<w_{n}
for all
n
, we deduce by a diagonal argument
that
(g_{k})
has a subsequence that converges pointwise to some
g\colon C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}\to\mathbb{R}
on
C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
and uniformly on closed subsets of
C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
.

To extend
g
to
[0,1]\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
, we set
g(x)=g(M_{L})
for all
x\in[0,M_{L})
and
g(x)=g(M_{R})
for all
x\in(M_{R},1]
if
m_{0}\in\Int C
, and otherwise set
g(x)=0
for all
x\in[0,1]\setminus C
if
m_{0}\notin\Int C
. Finally, by extracting a further subsequence
(g_{k_{\ell}})
if necessary, we can ensure that
g_{k_{\ell}}(\tilde{m}_{0})
converges to some
L\in[-\infty,\infty]
as
\ell\to\infty
, and we extend
g
to
[0,1]
by setting
g(\tilde{m}_{0})=L
.

(i) This follows from the construction of
g
in the first paragraph. Note also that if
m_{0}\notin\Int C
, then
g
is increasing on
C
, and
g
is either concave or convex on
C
,
depending on whether
\tilde{m}_{0}=M_{L}
or
\tilde{m}_{0}=M_{R}
respectively.

(ii) By the continuity of
g
on
C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
and the uniform convergence established above, we deduce that if
z_{\ell}\to z\in C\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
, then

\lvert g_{k_{\ell}}(z_{\ell})-g(z)\rvert\leq\lvert g_{k_{\ell}}(z_{\ell})-g(z_{\ell})\rvert+\lvert g(z_{\ell})-g(z)\rvert\to 0.

(S71)

(iii) If
\tilde{m}_{0}>M_{L}
, then
L=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}g_{k_{\ell}}(\tilde{m}_{0})\geq\lim_{x\nearrow\,\tilde{m}_{0}}\,\lim_{\ell\to\infty}g_{k_{\ell}}(x)=\lim_{x\nearrow\,\tilde{m}_{0}}g(x)
, so
L\in(-\infty,\infty]
. Similarly if
\tilde{m}_{0}<M_{R}
, then
L\leq\lim_{x\searrow\,\tilde{m}_{0}}g(x)
, whence
L\in[-\infty,\infty)
.

(iv) If
m_{0}\in\Int C
, then
g\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{0}}
by construction.
Suppose now that
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
. Since
Q_{\ell}\overset{d}{\to}P
under the hypotheses of Proposition
S12
, Skorokhod’s representation theorem
(e.g.
van der Vaart 1998
, Theorem 2.19)
guarantees the existence of random vectors
(X,Y),(X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2}),\dotsc
defined on a common probability space such that
(X,Y)\sim P
,
(X_{\ell},Y_{\ell})\sim Q_{\ell}
for all
\ell
and
(X_{\ell},Y_{\ell})\to(X,Y)
almost surely. Then it follows from (
S71
) that
g_{k_{\ell}}(X_{\ell})\to g(X)
almost surely on the event
\{X=\tilde{m}_{0}\}
, which has probability 1 since
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
by assumption. An application of Fatou’s lemma shows that

\displaystyle\infty>\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,L(f_{n},P_{n})\geq\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\,L(g_{k_{\ell}},Q_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\{Y_{\ell}-g_{k_{\ell}}(X_{\ell})\}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\,\{Y_{\ell}-g_{k_{\ell}}(X_{\ell})\}^{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\{Y-g(X)\}^{2}\bigr)=L(g,P).

(S72)

Thus,
\mathbb{E}\bigl(g(X)^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl(\{Y-g(X)\}^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}(Y^{2})^{1/2}<\infty
, so
g\in L^{2}(P^{X})
. By Proposition
S9
(d) and the proof of (i) above, it follows that
[g]_{P^{X}}\in\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(P^{X})
.

(v) Since
L(h_{n},P)\to L(h,P)
whenever
\|h_{n}-h\|_{L^{2}(P^{X})}\to 0
, it follows from (
S72
) and the conclusion of (iv) that
\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}L(g_{k_{\ell}},Q_{\ell})\geq L(g,P)\geq L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
, as required.

(vi) If
P_{n}=P
for all
n
, then we can modify the argument leading up to (
S72
) as follows: since
g_{k_{\ell}}\to g
pointwise on
C
and
P^{X}(C)=1
, it follows that if
(X,Y)\sim P
, then
g_{k_{\ell}}(X)\to g(X)
(almost surely) and
\infty>\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}L(g_{k_{\ell}},P)\geq L(g,P)
, as in (
S72
). Thus,
g\in L^{2}(P^{X})
and
[g]_{P^{X}}\in\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(P^{X})
as in the proof of (iv). In particular, when
\tilde{m}_{0}\notin\Int C
and
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})>0
, we must have
g(\tilde{m}_{0})\in\mathbb{R}
since
g\in L^{2}(P^{X})
. In this case, if
m_{0}\in C
, then
g\sim_{P^{X}}h
for some
h\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{0}}
, and otherwise if
m_{0}\notin C
, then
[g]_{P^{X}}\in\Cl\mathcal{F}_{P^{X}}^{m_{0}}
by Proposition
S9
(d). We conclude as before that
\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}L(g_{k_{\ell}},P)\geq L(g,P)\geq L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
in all cases, so the proof of Lemma
S28
is complete.
∎

Proof of Proposition
S12
.

(a) Fix
\varepsilon>0
. By Proposition
S9
(a) and the observation in the paragraph after (
S34
), there exists a continuous
h_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{0}}
such that
L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)\leq L(h_{0},P)\leq L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)+\varepsilon
. Now for
\eta\in[-m_{0},1-m_{0}]
, define
h_{\eta}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by
h_{\eta}(x):=h_{0}(0\vee(x-\eta)\wedge 1)
. Then
h_{\eta}\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{0}+\eta}
and
\sup_{x\in[0,1]}\,\lvert h_{\eta}(x)\rvert\leq\lvert h_{0}(0)\rvert\vee\lvert h_{0}(1)\rvert=:B
for all
\eta\in[-m_{0},1-m_{0}]
. In addition, it follows from Lemma
S30
that
h_{\eta}\to h_{0}
uniformly on
[0,1]
as
\eta\to 0
.
For each
n
, let
\eta_{n}:=m_{n}-m_{0}
, so that
h_{\eta_{n}}\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{n}}
and

L_{m_{n}}^{*}(P_{n})\leq L(h_{\eta_{n}},P_{n})=L(h_{0},P_{n})+\bigl(L(h_{\eta_{n}},P_{n})-L(h_{0},P_{n})\bigr)

by the definition of
L_{m_{n}}^{*}(P_{n})
. Observe that

\displaystyle\lvert L(h_{\eta_{n}},P_{n})-L(h_{0},P_{n})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\;\bigl|\bigl(y-h_{\eta_{n}}(x)\bigr)^{2}-\bigl(y-h_{0}(x)\bigr)^{2}\bigr|\,dP_{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\;\lvert h_{0}(x)-h_{\eta_{n}}(x)\rvert\,\bigl(2\lvert y\rvert+\lvert h_{0}(x)\rvert+\lvert h_{\eta_{n}}(x)\rvert\bigl)\,dP_{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in[0,1]}\,\lvert h_{0}(x)-h_{\eta_{n}}(x)\rvert\,\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\,2(\lvert y\rvert+B)\,dP_{n}(x,y).

Moreover,
W_{2}(P_{n},P)\to 0
by assumption, so
\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\|w\|^{2}\,dP_{n}(w)\to\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\|w\|^{2}\,dP(w)
. Thus, since
\bigl(y-h_{0}(x)\bigr)^{2}\leq 2(y^{2}+B^{2})
and
\lvert y\rvert\leq(1+y^{2})/2
for all
(x,y)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}
, we deduce using Lemma
S31
and the continuity of
h_{0}
that

\displaystyle L(h_{0},P_{n})=\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\,\bigl(y-h_{0}(x)\bigr)^{2}\,dP_{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle\to\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\,\bigl(y-h_{0}(x)\bigr)^{2}\,dP(x,y)=L(h_{0},P)

\displaystyle\text{and\quad}\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\,(\lvert y\rvert+B)\,dP_{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle\to\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}}\,(\lvert y\rvert+B)\,dP(x,y)<\infty

as
n\to\infty
. Since
h_{\eta_{n}}\to h_{0}
uniformly on
[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
, it follows from the above that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}L_{m_{n}}^{*}(P_{n})\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}L(h_{0},P_{n})=L(h_{0},P)\leq L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, this yields (a).

The proofs of (b)–(i) are based on the key Lemmas
S26
,
S27
and
S28
above.

(b) Fix a deterministic, non-negative sequence
(\delta_{n})
with
\delta_{n}\to 0
and let
(f_{n})
be any sequence with
f_{n}\in\psi_{m_{n}}^{\delta_{n}}(P_{n})
for all
n
, so that
L(f_{n},P_{n})\leq L_{m_{n}}^{*}(P_{n})+\delta_{n}
for all
n
. Then
\limsup_{n\to\infty}L(f_{n},P_{n})=\limsup_{n\to\infty}L_{m_{n}}^{*}(P_{n})\leq L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)<\infty
by (a), so
(f_{n})
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
S28
. In particular, Lemma
S28
(v) applies to every subsequence of
(f_{n})
when
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
, in which case

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\,L_{m_{n}}^{*}(P_{n})=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\,L(f_{n},P_{n})\geq L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P).

(S73)

(c) If in addition
P_{n}=P
for all
n
, then Lemma
S28
(vi) applies to every subsequence of
(f_{n})
, regardless of whether or not
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
, so (
S73
) holds in all cases. Together with part (a) above, this establishes (c) and the fact that
L^{*}(P)
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)
are well-defined.

(d) If
P^{X}(\{m\})=0
for all
m\in[0,1]
, then (a) and (b) imply that for any sequence
(m_{n}^{\prime})
in
[0,1]
converging to some
m^{\prime}\in[0,1]
, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{m_{n}^{\prime}}^{*}(P_{n})=L_{m^{\prime}}^{*}(P)
. In other words, the functions
m\mapsto L_{m}^{*}(P_{n})
converge continuously to
m\mapsto L_{m}^{*}(P)
on
[0,1]
in the sense of
Remmert 1991
. Since continuous convergence is equivalent to uniform convergence on the compact space
[0,1]

(e.g.
Remmert 1991
, pages 98–99)
, the first part of (d) follows. This in turn implies the second assertion that
L^{*}(P_{n})=\min_{m\in[0,1]}L_{m}^{*}(P_{n})\to\min_{m\in[0,1]}L_{m}^{*}(P)=L^{*}(P)
as
n\to\infty
.

(e) Let
(m_{n}^{\prime})
be any sequence in
[0,1]
with
m_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{\delta_{n}}(P_{n})
for all
n
. For each subsequence of
(m_{n}^{\prime})
, we may extract a further subsequence
(m_{n_{k}}^{\prime})
that converges to some
m^{\prime}\in[0,1]
.
For each
k
, we have
L^{*}(P_{n_{k}})\leq L_{m_{n_{k}}^{\prime}}^{*}(P_{n_{k}})\leq L^{*}(P_{n_{k}})+\delta_{n_{k}}
by the definition of
\mathcal{I}^{\delta_{n_{k}}}(P_{n_{k}})
. Thus, if
P^{X}(\{m\})=0
for all
m\in[0,1]
, then for any
m^{*}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)
, we deduce from (a) and (b) that

L^{*}(P)\leq L_{m^{\prime}}^{*}(P)=\lim_{k\to\infty}L_{m_{n_{k}}^{\prime}}^{*}(P_{n_{k}})=\lim_{k\to\infty}L^{*}(P_{n_{k}})\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}L_{m^{*}}^{*}(P_{n_{k}})=L_{m^{*}}^{*}(P)=L^{*}(P),

so
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)
and
L^{*}(P_{n_{k}})\to L^{*}(P)
as
k\to\infty
. This shows that every subsequence of
(m_{n}^{\prime})
has a further subsequence that converges to an element of
\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)
. Since
(m_{n}^{\prime})
was arbitrary, this implies (e). Similarly, every subsequence of
\bigl(L^{*}(P_{n})\bigr)
has a further subsequence that converges to
L^{*}(P)
; this is another way to obtain the second part of (d).

For (f)–(i), fix any sequence
(f_{n})
with
f_{n}\in\psi_{m_{n}}^{\delta_{n}}(P_{n})
for all
n
. If
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
, then for any subsequence
(g_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\equiv(f_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
of
(f_{n})
, we can find a further subsequence
(g_{k_{\ell}})
and a function
g
on
[0,1]
satisfying conditions (i)–(v) in Lemma
S28
. In particular, setting
m_{\ell}^{\prime}:=m_{n_{k_{\ell}}}
and
Q_{\ell}:=P_{n_{k_{\ell}}}
, we deduce from (a) and Lemma
S28
(v) that

L(g,P)\leq\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\,L(g_{k_{\ell}},Q_{\ell})\leq\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\,L(g_{k_{\ell}},Q_{\ell})=\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\,L_{m_{\ell}^{\prime}}^{*}(Q_{\ell})\leq L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P),

where the equality above follows from the fact that
L(g_{k_{\ell}},Q_{\ell})\leq L_{m_{\ell}^{\prime}}^{*}(Q_{\ell})+\delta_{m_{k_{\ell}}}
for all
\ell
. We conclude from Lemma
S28
(iv) and Corollary
S10
(c) that
g\in\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{*}(P)=\psi_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
.

(f) For each closed set
A\subseteq(\supp P^{X})\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
, Lemma
S28
(ii) asserts that
g_{k_{\ell}}\to g\in\psi_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
uniformly on
A
. Thus, every subsequence of
(f_{n})
has a further subsequence that converges uniformly on
A
to an element of
\psi_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
, and by Corollary
S10
(a), all elements of
\psi_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
agree
P^{X}
-almost everywhere on
A
. Since
(f_{n})
was arbitrary, (f) follows.

(g) If
\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{0}(P)\neq\emptyset
, then by Corollary
S10
(a), there exists
f^{*}\in\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{0}(P)
such that
\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{*}(P)=[f^{*}]_{P^{X}}
, so
g\sim_{P^{X}}f^{*}
in the argument before (f). Thus, in view of Lemma
S28
(i), we may assume that
g\in\mathcal{F}^{\tilde{m}_{0}}
, so that
g\in\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{0}(P)
. By applying Lemma
S28
(ii) as above, we deduce that for any closed set
A\subseteq(\supp P^{X})\setminus\{\tilde{m}_{0}\}
, every subsequence of
(f_{n})
has a further subsequence that converges uniformly on
A
to an element of
\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{0}(P)
. All functions in
\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{0}(P)
agree on
A
by Corollary
S10
(e), and
(f_{n})
was arbitrary, so (g) holds.

Suppose in addition that
m_{0}\in\Int(\csupp P^{X})
and
P^{X}(\{m_{0}\})=0
. Then in the argument before (f), we can insist that
g\in\mathcal{F}^{m_{0}}
in view of Lemma
S28
(iv), so that
g\in\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P)
.

(h) For fixed
q\in[1,\infty)
, Lemma
S27
implies that there exists
\tilde{B}<\infty
such that
\lvert g_{k_{\ell}}-g\rvert^{q}\leq 2^{q-1}(\tilde{B}^{q}+\lvert g\rvert^{q})
on
[0,1]
for all sufficiently large
\ell
, so
\|g_{k_{\ell}}-g\|_{L^{q}(P^{X})}^{q}=\int_{[0,1]}\,\lvert g_{k_{\ell}}-g\rvert^{q}\,dP^{X}\to 0
by the dominated convergence theorem. In summary, every subsequence of
\bigl([f_{n}]_{P^{X}}\bigr)_{n=1}^{\infty}
has a further subsequence that converges in
\mathcal{L}^{q}(P^{X})
to
\psi_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
, so the entire sequence converges in
\mathcal{L}^{q}(P^{X})
to
\psi_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)
, as required.

(i) Under the hypotheses of (i), all elements of
\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P)
are continuous by Corollary
S10
(f), so we can apply Lemmas
S28
(ii) and
S30
to obtain the stronger conclusion that every subsequence of
(f_{n})
has a further subsequence that converges uniformly on
\csupp P^{X}
to some function in
\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P)
. Since elements of
\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P)
agree on
\supp P^{X}
by assumption and
(f_{n})
was arbitrary, (i) holds.
∎

Proof of Corollary
S14
.

(a) By definition, we have
\psi^{0}(P)=\{f\in\mathcal{F}:L(f,P)=L^{*}(P)\}=\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)}\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
. If
\psi_{m}^{0}(P)\neq\emptyset
for some
m\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)\setminus\csupp P^{X}
, then
\tilde{m}=\argmin_{x\in\csupp P^{X}}\lvert x-m\rvert
satisfies
\tilde{m}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)\cap\csupp P^{X}
and
\psi_{\tilde{m}}^{0}(P)\neq\emptyset
by Corollary
S10
(c). The result now follows from Corollary
S10
(d).

For (b)–(d), fix a sequence
(f_{n})
with
f_{n}\in\psi^{\delta_{n}}(P_{n})
for all
n
, so that there exists a sequence
(m_{n})
in
[0,1]
with
m_{n}\in\mathcal{I}^{\delta_{n}}(P_{n})
and
f_{n}\in\psi_{m_{n}}^{\delta_{n}}(P_{n})
for all
n
. By assumption, we have
P^{X}(\{m\})=0
for all
m\in[0,1]
, so for each subsequence of
(f_{n})
, Proposition
S12
(e) ensures the existence of a further subsequence
(f_{n_{k}})
such that
m_{n_{k}}\to m^{*}
for some
m^{*}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)
. Let
\tilde{m}^{*}:=\argmin_{x\in\csupp P^{X}}\,\lvert m^{*}-x\rvert
. Then
L_{\tilde{m}^{*}}^{*}(P)=L_{m^{*}}^{*}(P)=L^{*}(P)
by Corollary
S10
(c), so
\tilde{m}^{*}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)
. We are now in a position to apply Proposition
S12
(f)–(i).

(b) Fix a closed set
A\subseteq(\supp P^{X})\setminus\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)\subseteq(\supp P^{X})\setminus\{\tilde{m}^{*}\}
. For any
f^{*}\in\psi_{m^{*}}^{*}(P)\subseteq\psi^{*}(P)
, Proposition
S12
(f) implies that
\|f_{n_{k}}-f^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}(A,P^{X})}\to 0
. Thus, every subsequence of
(f_{n})
has a further subsequence that converges in
\|{\cdot}\|_{L^{\infty}(A,P^{X})}
to an element of
\psi^{*}(P)
. Since
(f_{n})
was arbitrary, this yields (b).

(c) Fix a closed set
A\subseteq(\supp P^{X})\setminus\tilde{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(P)
.

•

If
\tilde{m}^{*}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(P)
, then
\tilde{m}^{*}=m^{*}\in\Int(\csupp P^{X})
and all elements of
\psi_{\tilde{m}^{*}}^{0}(P)=\psi_{m^{*}}^{0}(P)\neq\emptyset
are continuous, so for any
f^{*}\in\psi_{m^{*}}^{0}(P)\subseteq\psi^{0}(P)
, Proposition
S12
(i) implies that
f_{n_{k}}\to f^{*}
uniformly on
\supp P^{X}\supseteq A
.

•

If
\tilde{m}^{*}\in\tilde{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(P)
, then we still have
\tilde{m}^{*}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)\cap\csupp P^{X}
, so
\psi_{\tilde{m}^{*}}^{0}(P)\neq\emptyset
by assumption. Thus, for any
f^{*}\in\psi_{\tilde{m}^{*}}^{0}(P)\subseteq\psi^{0}(P)
, Proposition
S12
(g) implies that
f_{n_{k}}\to f^{*}
uniformly on
(\supp P^{X})\setminus\{\tilde{m}^{*}\}\supseteq A
.

Thus, every subsequence of
(f_{n})
has a further subsequence that converges uniformly on
A
to an element of
\psi^{0}(P)
. Since
(f_{n})
was arbitrary, (c) follows.

(d) Here,
m^{*}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)\subseteq\Int(\csupp P^{X})
by assumption, so for any
f^{*}\in\psi_{m^{*}}^{0}(P)\subseteq\psi^{*}(P)
, Proposition
S12
(h) implies that
\|f_{n_{k}}-f^{*}\|_{L^{q}(P^{X})}\to 0
for any
q\in[1,\infty)
. By the same reasoning as in (b, c), we obtain (d).
∎

Proof of Proposition
S16
.

In the definition of
P_{0}
, we have
\mathbb{E}(f_{0}(X)+\xi\,|\,X)=f_{0}(X)
since
\xi
is independent of
X
and has mean 0 by (ii), so
f_{0}
is a regression function for
P_{0}
(in the sense of (
S34
) in Section
2
). It now follows from condition (iii) and Corollary
S10
(d) that
\psi_{m}^{0}(P_{0})\neq\emptyset
for all
m\in\csupp P_{0}^{X}
, so in particular
\psi^{0}(P_{0})\neq\emptyset
.

Writing
(D_{n})
for any one of the sequences of random variables in (a)–(d), we aim to prove that
D_{n}\overset{p}{\to}0
, or equivalently that every subsequence
(D_{n_{k}})
has a further subsequence that converges almost surely to 0. If it can be shown that
W_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n},P_{0})\overset{p}{\to}0
, then we can take
(D_{n_{k_{\ell}}})
to be a subsequence of
(D_{n_{k}})
such that
W_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n_{k_{\ell}}},P_{0})\to 0
almost surely. Working on an event
\Omega_{0}
of probability 1 on which this convergence takes place, we deduce directly from the relevant parts of Proposition
S12
or Corollary
S14
that
D_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\!\to 0
on
\Omega_{0}
; note that assertions (a, b) follow from Proposition
S12
(d, e) and assertions (c, d) follow from Corollary
S14
(c, d).

To complete the proof, we must therefore verify that
W_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n},P_{0})\overset{p}{\to}0
under conditions (i)–(iii). It suffices to show that

\text{($\ast$)}\;\;\mathbb{P}_{n}\overset{d}{\to}P_{0}\text{ almost surely}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{($\ast\ast$)}\;\;\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\|w\|^{2}\,d\mathbb{P}_{n}(w)\overset{p}{\to}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\|w\|^{2}\,dP_{0}(w),

since then we can argue along subsequences of
(\mathbb{P}_{n})
as in the previous paragraph.

(
\ast
) Let
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{n}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{(x_{ni},\,\xi_{ni})}
for each
n
and define
\tilde{P}_{0}:=P_{0}^{X}\otimes P_{\xi}
. Defining the map
F_{0}\colon(x,z)\mapsto(x,f_{0}(x)+z)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we therefore have
\mathbb{P}_{n}=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{n}\circ F_{0}^{-1}
for each
n
and
P_{0}=\tilde{P}_{0}\circ F_{0}^{-1}
. The desired convergence statement (
\ast
) follows from the following two claims.

Claim 3
.

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{n}\overset{d}{\to}\tilde{P}_{0}
almost surely.

Proof of Claim.

By
Billingsley 1999
, the countable set
\mathcal{R}:=\{[a_{1},b_{1}]\times[a_{2},b_{2}]:a_{j}\leq b_{j}\text{ and }a_{j},b_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}\text{ for }j=1,2\}
is a convergence-determining class in the sense of
Billingsley 1999
, so it suffices to show that
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{n}(R)\to\tilde{P}_{0}(R)
almost surely for each
R\in\mathcal{R}
. To this end, fix any
I_{1}\times I_{2}\in\mathcal{R}
, where
I_{j}=[a_{j},b_{j}]
is an interval with rational endpoints
a_{j}\leq b_{j}
for
j=1,2
. By condition (i),
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}\overset{d}{\to}P_{0}^{X}
and
P_{0}^{X}(\{a_{1},b_{1}\})=0
, so
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}(I_{1})\to P_{0}^{X}(I_{1})
as
n\to\infty
. For each
n
, defining
r_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x_{ni}\in I_{1}\}}=n\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}(I_{1})
, we can write
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{n}(I_{1}\times I_{2})=\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}(I_{1})\,T_{n}
, where

T_{n}:=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x_{ni}\in I_{1}\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\xi_{ni}\in I_{2}\}}}{r_{n}\vee 1}\sim\frac{1}{r_{n}\vee 1}\Bin\bigl(r_{n},P_{\xi}(I_{2})\bigr).

If
P_{0}^{X}(I_{1})=0
, then certainly
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{n}(I_{1}\times I_{2})=\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}(I_{1})\,T_{n}\to 0=P_{0}^{X}(I_{1})P_{\xi}(I_{2})=\tilde{P}_{0}(I_{1}\times I_{2})
. On the other hand, if
P_{0}^{X}(I_{1})>0
, then
r_{n}=n\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr)P_{0}^{X}(I_{1})\to\infty
, so for all
t>0
, we have
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\lvert T_{n}-P_{\xi}(I_{2})\rvert>t)<\infty
by Hoeffding’s inequality (or some other suitable exponential tail bound for binomial random variables; see
van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
for example). Thus, by the first Borel–Cantelli lemma,
T_{n}\to P_{\xi}(I_{2})
almost surely, so
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{n}(I_{1}\times I_{2})=\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}(I_{1})\,T_{n}\to P_{0}^{X}(I_{1})P_{\xi}(I_{2})=\tilde{P}_{0}(I_{1}\times I_{2})
almost surely as
n\to\infty
, as required.
∎

Claim 4
.

If
(Q_{n})
is any sequence of probability measures such that
Q_{n}\overset{d}{\to}\tilde{P}_{0}
, then
Q_{n}\circ F_{0}^{-1}\overset{d}{\to}\tilde{P}_{0}\circ F_{0}^{-1}=P_{0}
under condition (iii).

Proof of Claim.

It follows from Skorokhod’s representation theorem
(e.g.
van der Vaart 1998
, Theorem 2.19)
that there exist random vectors
(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z}),(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{Z}_{1}),(\tilde{X}_{2},\tilde{Z}_{2}),\dotsc
defined on a common probability space such that
(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})\sim\tilde{P}_{0}
,
(\tilde{X}_{n},\tilde{Z}_{n})\sim Q_{n}
for all
n
and
(\tilde{X}_{n},\tilde{Z}_{n})\to(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})
almost surely.

Since
\tilde{X}\sim P_{0}^{X}
and
f_{0}
is continuous
P_{0}^{X}
-almost everywhere under condition (iii), we have
f_{0}(\tilde{X}_{n})\to f_{0}(\tilde{X})
almost surely, so
F_{0}(\tilde{X}_{n},\tilde{Z}_{n})=(\tilde{X}_{n},f_{0}(\tilde{X}_{n})+\tilde{Z}_{n})\to(\tilde{X},f_{0}(\tilde{X})+\tilde{Z})=F_{0}(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})
almost surely. Thus, the distribution
Q_{n}\circ F_{0}^{-1}
of
F_{0}(\tilde{X}_{n},\tilde{Z}_{n})
converges weakly to the distribution
\tilde{P}_{0}\circ F_{0}^{-1}=P_{0}
of
F_{0}(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})
, as required.
∎

(
\ast\ast
) Let
X\sim P_{0}^{X}
and
\xi\sim P_{\xi}
be independent, so that
\bigl(X,f_{0}(X)+\xi\bigr)\sim P_{0}
. We have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\|w\|^{2}\,d\mathbb{P}_{n}(w)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{ni}^{2}+Y_{ni}^{2})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(x_{ni}^{2}+f_{0}^{2}(x_{ni})+2f_{0}(x_{ni})\,\xi_{ni}+\xi_{ni}^{2}\bigr)

(S74)

and now consider each of the summands on the right-hand side. Since
(\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X})
is a sequence of probability measures on the compact set
[0,1]
satisfying
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}\overset{d}{\to}P_{0}^{X}
, we have
W_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X},P_{0}^{X})\to 0
, so

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{ni}^{2}=\int_{[0,1]}x^{2}\,d\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}(x)\to\int_{[0,1]}x^{2}\,dP_{0}^{X}(x)=\mathbb{E}(X^{2}).

(S75)

In addition, we can apply condition (iii) and argue as in the proof of the first Claim to see that
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}\circ f_{0}^{-1}\overset{d}{\to}P_{0}^{X}\circ f_{0}^{-1}
. Since
f_{0}
is bounded on
[0,1]
by (iii), these probability measures are also supported on some compact set, so in fact
W_{2}\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}\circ f_{0}^{-1},P_{0}^{X}\circ f_{0}^{-1}\bigr)\to 0
, whence

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}^{2}(x_{ni})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}\,d\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{n}^{X}\circ f_{0}^{-1}\bigr)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}\,d\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{X}\circ f_{0}^{-1}\bigr)(x)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{0}^{2}(X)\bigr).

(S76)

For all
t>0
, it follows from (
S76
), condition (ii) and Chebsyhev’s inequality that

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\,\biggl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(x_{ni})\,\xi_{ni}\biggr|>t\biggr)\leq\frac{1}{(nt)^{2}}\Var\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(x_{ni})\,\xi_{ni}\biggr)=\frac{1}{(nt)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}^{2}(x_{ni})\Var(\xi)\to 0,

(S77)

so
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(x_{ni})\,\xi_{ni}\overset{p}{\to}0=\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{0}(X)\xi\bigr)
by the independence of
X
and
\xi
. Finally, by condition (ii),
\xi_{n1},\dotsc,\xi_{nn}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}P_{\xi}
for each
n
, so
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{ni}^{2}\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})
by the weak law of large numbers. Together with (
S74
), (
S75
), (
S76
) and (
S77
), this implies that

\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\|w\|^{2}\,d\mathbb{P}_{n}(w)\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{E}(X^{2})+\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{0}^{2}(X)\bigr)+2\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{0}(X)\xi\bigr)+\mathbb{E}(\xi^{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\|w\|^{2}\,dP_{0}(w),

so (
\ast\ast
) holds.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2
.

The results for
(\tilde{g}_{n})=(\tilde{f}_{n})
follow from Proposition
S16
. When
(\tilde{g}_{n})=(\hat{f}_{n}^{m_{0}})
, we again have
W_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n},P_{0})\overset{p}{\to}0
under conditions (i)–(iii) in Proposition
S16
, so assertions (a, b, c, d) follow from Proposition
S12
(e, g, i, h) in this case.
∎

S7
Auxiliary results and examples for Section
S3

The proofs in Section
S6
make use of two straightforward results on the convergence of sequences of S-shaped functions.

Lemma S29
.

Suppose that
(f_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of increasing convex functions on
[0,1)
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(x)
exists for all
x\in(0,1)
. Then
\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(0)
exists and the function
f\colon[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
f(x):=\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(x)
is convex and increasing.
Moreover,
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on
[0,w]
for every
w\in[0,1)
.

Proof.

Since
\left.f_{n}\right|_{(0,1)}
is convex and increasing for all
n
, the same is true of the pointwise limit
\left.f\right|_{(0,1)}
. Thus,
l:=\lim_{t\,\searrow\,0}f(t)
exists and is finite, and we now show that
f_{n}(0)\to l
as
n\to\infty
. Since each
f_{n}
is convex on
[0,1)
, we have
f_{n}(0)\geq 2f_{n}(t)-f_{n}(2t)
for all
t>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so

\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(0)\geq\lim_{t\,\searrow\,0}\,\lim_{n\to\infty}\,\bigl(2f_{n}(t)-f_{n}(2t)\bigr)=\lim_{t\,\searrow\,0}\,\bigl(2f(t)-f(2t)\bigr)=l.

On the other hand,
f_{n}
is increasing on
[0,1)
for all
n
, so

\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(0)\leq\lim_{t\,\searrow\,0}\,\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(t)=\lim_{t\,\searrow\,0}f(t)=l,

which means that
f(0)=l
, as required. Consequently,
f
is convex and increasing on
[0,1)
. For the final assertion of the lemma, we extend
f,f_{1},f_{2},\dotsc
to increasing convex functions on
(-\infty,1)
by setting
f(x)=f(0)
and
f_{n}(x)=f_{n}(0)
for all
x<0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows from what we have already shown that
f_{n}\to f
pointwise on
(-\infty,1)
, so in fact
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on compact subsets of
(-\infty,1)
by
Rockafellar 1997
. This yields the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma S30
.

If
m\in(0,1)
and
(f_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of functions in
\mathcal{F}^{m}
that converges pointwise on
[0,1]\setminus\{m\}
to some continuous
f\in\mathcal{F}^{m}
, then
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on
[0,1]
.

Proof.

First note that
f_{n}\to f
pointwise on
[0,1]
. Indeed,

f(z)=\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(z)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(m)\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(m)\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(w)=f(w)

whenever
z<m<w
, and since
\lim_{z\nearrow\,m}f(z)=f(m)=\lim_{w\searrow\,m}f(w)
by continuity, it follows that
f_{n}(m)\to f(m)
. We now show that
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on
[0,1]
by a standard argument:
fix
\varepsilon>0
and note that since
f
is continuous and increasing on
[0,1]
, we can find
0=z_{0}<z_{1}<\dotsc<z_{k-1}<z_{k}=1
such that
f(z_{i})-f(z_{i-1})<\varepsilon
for all
i\in[k]
. Since each
f_{n}
is increasing, we see that if
x\in[z_{i-1},z_{i}]
, then

f_{n}(z_{i-1})-f(z_{i-1})-\varepsilon<f_{n}(z_{i-1})-f(z_{i})\leq f_{n}(x)-f(x)\leq f_{n}(z_{i})-f(z_{i-1})<f_{n}(z_{i})-f(z_{i})+\varepsilon.

Since
f_{n}\to f
pointwise,
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in[0,1]}\,\lvert f_{n}(x)-f(x)\rvert<\lim_{n\to\infty}\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\,\lvert f_{n}(z_{i})-f(z_{i})\rvert+\varepsilon=\varepsilon
. This holds for all
\varepsilon>0
, so the result follows.
∎

The weak convergence result below is stated as Lemma 4.5 in
Dümbgen et al. 2011
and proved here for completeness.

Lemma S31
.

Let
P,P_{1},P_{2},\dotsc
be probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
P_{n}\overset{d}{\to}P
. If
h
is a non-negative, continuous function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h\,dP_{n}\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h\,dP<\infty
, then
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,dP_{n}\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,dP
for any continuous function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\|f\|/(1+h)
is bounded.

Proof.

We can restrict attention to the case
k=1
since the component functions can be considered separately when
k>1
. Let
C>0
be such that
\lvert f\rvert\leq C(1+h)
pointwise. For a Borel measure
Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a
Q
-integrable function
h
, we write
Q(h)
as shorthand for
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h\,dQ
.

Since
P_{n}\overset{d}{\to}P
, we have
\liminf_{n\to\infty}P_{n}(g)\geq P(g)
for all non-negative, continuous
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by the portmanteau lemma
(e.g.
van der Vaart 1998
, Lemma 2.2(iv))
.
Thus, since
f^{+}:=f\vee 0
and
C(1+h)-f^{+}
are non-negative and continuous, we have
\liminf_{n\to\infty}P_{n}(f^{+})\geq P(f^{+})
and
\liminf_{n\to\infty}P_{n}\bigl(C(1+h)-f^{+}\bigr)\geq P\bigl(C(1+h)-f^{+}\bigr)
. Moreover,
P_{n}\bigl(C(1+h)\bigr)\to P\bigl(C(1+h)\bigr)
by assumption, so in fact
P_{n}(f^{+})\to P(f^{+})
. A similar argument shows that
P_{n}(f^{-})\to P(f^{-})
, where
f^{-}:=(-f)\vee 0
, so we indeed have
P_{n}(f)\to P(f)
.
∎

We conclude this subsection with a series of related examples which illustrate that some of the assertions of Proposition
S12
and Corollary
S14
do not hold in general if the associated technical conditions are not satisfied.

Example S32
.

We first consider situations where either
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})>0
or
m_{0}\notin\Int(\csupp P^{X})
. In each of the following, we construct
P\in\mathcal{P}
and a sequence
(P_{n})
in
\mathcal{P}
with
W_{2}(P_{n},P)\to 0
, where
P=(1-\eta)Q+\eta\tilde{Q}
and
P_{n}=(1-\eta_{n})Q_{n}+\eta_{n}\tilde{Q}_{n}
for suitable
Q,Q_{n},\tilde{Q}_{n}\in\mathcal{P}
and
\eta,\eta_{n}\in[0,1]
. For
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
\delta_{w}
for a point mass at
w
.

(a)

Fix
m_{0}\in(0,1]
and
m\in(0,m_{0}]
. Let
Q_{n}:=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(0,0)}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(m(1-1/n),\,0)}
,
Q:=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(0,0)}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(m,0)}
,
\tilde{Q}_{n}=\tilde{Q}:=\delta_{(m,1)}
and
\eta_{n}=\eta:=1/3
for all
n
. Then
P_{n}=\frac{1}{3}\delta_{(0,0)}+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{(m(1-1/n),\,0)}+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{(m,1)}\overset{d}{\to}\frac{1}{3}\delta_{(0,0)}+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{(m,0)}+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{(m,1)}=P
and
P^{X}=\frac{1}{3}\delta_{0}+\frac{2}{3}\delta_{m}
, so
m_{0}\notin\Int(\csupp P^{X})
,
\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)=[0,1]\not\subseteq\Int(\csupp P^{X})
,
\tilde{m}_{0}=m
and
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})>0
. Since
P,P_{1},P_{2},\dotsc
are supported on the compact set
[0,1]^{2}
, we automatically have
W_{2}(P_{n},P)\to 0
.

We claim that Propositions
S12
(b, d, h) and Corollary
S14
(d) do not apply here. Indeed, for each
n
, we have
f_{n}(m)=1
for all
f_{n}\in\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P_{n})=\psi^{0}(P_{n})
and
L^{*}(P_{n})=L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P_{n})=0
, whereas
f^{*}(m)=1/2
for all
f^{*}\in\psi_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)=\psi^{*}(P)
and
L^{*}(P)=L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)=1/6>0=\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{m_{0}}^{*}(P_{n})
. Thus, if
f_{n}\in\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P_{n})
for all
n
, then
\lvert f_{n}(m)-f^{*}(m)\rvert=1/2
for all
n
and
f^{*}\in\psi^{*}(P)
, so
\inf_{f^{*}\in\psi^{*}(P)}\|f_{n}-f^{*}\|_{L^{q}(P^{X})}\nrightarrow 0
for
q\in[1,\infty)
.

(b)

This is a variant of (a) with
m=m_{0}\in\Int(\csupp P^{X})=(0,1)
but
P^{X}(\{m_{0}\})>0
. Let
Q_{n},Q
and
\eta_{n},\eta
be as in (a) but instead define
\tilde{Q}_{n}=\tilde{Q}:=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(m_{0},1)}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{(1,1)}
. Then
P^{X}=\frac{1}{3}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{m_{0}}+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{1}
and all the deductions in the second paragraph of (a) remain valid here.

(c)

Fix
m_{0}\in(0,1]
and
m\in(0,m_{0}]
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
Q,Q_{n}
be the uniform distributions on
\{(x,0):0\leq x\leq m\}
and
\{(x,0):0\leq x\leq m(1-1/n)\}
respectively. Moreover, let
\tilde{Q}:=Q
,
\eta:=0
,
\tilde{Q}_{n}:=\delta_{(m,n^{2})}
and
\eta_{n}:=n^{-5}
for all
n
. Then
P_{n}=(1-\eta_{n})Q_{n}+\eta_{n}\tilde{Q}_{n}
converges in
W_{2}
to
P=Q
. Indeed, for any continuous
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\lvert f(x,y)\rvert\leq 1+x^{2}+y^{2}
for all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have
\eta_{n}f(m,n^{2})=O(1/n)
, so
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f\,dP_{n}=(1-\eta_{n})\int_{0}^{m(1-1/n)}f(x,0)\,dx+\eta_{n}f(m,n^{2})\to\int_{0}^{m}f(x,0)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f\,dP
as
n\to\infty
.

Here,
\tilde{m}_{0}=m
and
P^{X}(\{m^{\prime}\})=0
for all
m^{\prime}\in[0,1]
, but
m_{0}\notin\Int(\csupp P^{X})
,
\mathcal{I}^{*}(P)=[0,1]\not\subseteq\Int(\csupp P^{X})
, and Proposition
S12
(h) and Corollary
S14
(d) do not hold. Indeed, for each
n
, let
f_{n}\in\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P_{n})=\psi^{0}(P_{n})
be such that
f_{n}=0
on
[0,m(1-1/n)]
,
f_{n}(m)=n^{2}
and
f_{n}
is linear on
[m(1-1/n),1]
. Since
f^{*}=0

P^{X}
-almost everywhere on
[0,m]
for all
f^{*}\in\psi_{m_{0}}^{*}(P)=\psi^{*}(P)
, we have
\inf_{f^{*}\in\psi^{*}(P)}\|f_{n}-f^{*}\|_{L^{q}(P^{X})}=\inf_{f^{*}\in\psi^{*}(P)}\|f_{n}\|_{L^{q}(P^{X})}\geq\inf_{f^{*}\in\psi^{*}(P)}\|f_{n}\|_{L^{1}(P^{X})}=n\to\infty
for all
q\in[1,\infty)
.

In Proposition
S12
(i), the assumption that
\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P)\neq\emptyset
and all elements of
\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P)
agree on
\supp P^{X}
is clearly necessary for the conclusion to hold (not least when
P_{n}=P
for all
n
). When this condition is not satisfied, some elements of
\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P)
are discontinuous by Corollary
S10
(e). If in addition
m_{0}\in\Int(\csupp P^{X})\cap\supp P^{X}
and
P^{X}(\{m_{0}\})=0
, then Proposition
S12
(i) fails. This is demonstrated by the next example, which is a modification of Example
S32
(b).

Example S33
.

Fix
m\in(0,1)
and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
f_{n}\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
be such that
f_{n}=0
on
[0,m(1-1/n)]
,
f_{n}=1
on
[m,1]
, and
f_{n}
is continuous and linear on
[m(1-1/n),m]
. For each
n
, let
P_{n}
be the distribution supported on
\{(x,f_{n}(x)):x\in[0,1]\}
for which the corresponding marginal distribution
P_{n}^{X}
is the uniform distribution on
D:=[0,m]\cup[m^{\prime},1]
for some
m^{\prime}\in[m,1)
. Then
(P_{n})
converges in
W_{2}
to the uniform distribution
P
on
\{(x,\mathbbm{1}_{[m,1]}(x)):x\in D\}
.

Not all elements of
\psi^{0}(P)=\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
agree at
m\in\supp P^{X}
; for example when
m^{\prime}=m
and
D=[0,1]
, the functions in
\psi_{m}^{0}(P)
agree with
\mathbbm{1}_{[m,1]}
on
[0,1]\setminus\{m\}
but can take any value in
[0,1]
at
m
. We have
P^{X}(\{m\})=0
and
m\in\Int(\csupp P^{X})
, so the other conditions of Proposition
S12
(i) are satisfied, but
\psi^{0}(P_{n})=\psi_{m}^{0}(P_{n})=\{f_{n}\}
for all
n
and
(f_{n})
does not have a uniform limit on
[0,m]\subseteq\supp P^{X}
.

In Example
S33
, note in addition that while all the conditions of Corollary
S14
(c) are met, the associated convergence statement cannot be extended to
A=\supp P^{X}
. In general, Proposition
S12
(g) also does not hold with
A=\supp P^{X}
under the stated conditions
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
and
\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{0}(P)\neq\emptyset
.
To see this, we can instead take
D=[0,m]
in Example
S33
and fix
m_{0}\in(m,1]
, so that
\tilde{m}_{0}=m
,
P^{X}(\{\tilde{m}_{0}\})=0
and
0\in\psi_{\tilde{m}_{0}}^{0}(P)
. Then there is a sequence
(g_{n})
with
g_{n}\in\psi_{m_{0}}^{0}(P_{n})
for all
n
but no uniform limit on
\supp P^{X}=[0,m]
; for example, let
g_{n}
be such that
g_{n}=0
on
[0,m(1-1/n)]
,
g_{n}(m)=1
and
g_{n}
is linear on
[m(1-1/n),1]
.
